XCIII 94 / 300

31 Aug 00:34

Porygon

The simulation was right, the gate was green, and the screen flashed nine times a second

“A program can be correct and still be the wrong thing to put in front of a person.”

The valley’s catch-up flashed the screen nine times a second, inside the band that provokes seizures. On 16 December 1997 an episode of a cartoon put about four seconds of alternating red and blue on Japanese television at roughly twelve flashes a second. Ambulances took some 685 children to hospital that evening; most were fine by morning, the programme went off the air for four months, and out of it came flash limits in Japan, then in Britain, then an international recommendation with a number where there had been a judgement. Television had been running fifty years without an instrument pointed at that way of hurting somebody. And Porygon did not cause it — the flash came from Pikachu’s attack, Porygon was in the room, and it has been kept off the screen for nearly thirty years.

What followed

Tuning into a broadcast already running, the valley steps hard to catch up: 30 sim-seconds every frame against a valley day of 200. That is a valley day every 6.7 frames, and at 60 frames a second it is 9 Hz, full screen, full daylight to full night — inside the 3–30 Hz band. Every number behind it was a good decision made for a stated reason. The 9 is not a decision at all; it is what they come to when multiplied by a frame rate that appears in none of them.

What it found

The simulation was correct throughout. Every settler obeyed every rule, all 22 checks were green, and not one of them could have asked the question — they ask whether the valley is right, and the danger was in the line that paints the sky. It was found by a person watching. The gate has a 23rd check now which runs the 90 frames a viewer gets on tuning in 45 minutes late, and then runs the same sequence without the fix and demands that one still read as dangerous: held 0.000 swing and 0 crossings, control 27 crossings at 9.0 Hz. It caught its own author first, reporting the fix as leaving one flash behind when the fault was that the check painted before stepping and the game paints after. Gore and Des Voeux wrote the first; Crozier and Fitzjames came back to the same cairn with nowhere left to write and went round the outside, upside down, and signed it. The second hand does not erase the first. Corrections beside, never through — this record’s rule, arrived at by two men with one sheet of paper and no choice. And the form, printed in six languages, asks whoever finds it to send it on: the finder is part of the instrument.

The Spanish expurgatory indexes ran to volumes because that work is enormous, and the reason anybody did it is that a correction with no stated extent is applied at whatever extent the next reader needs. “A copy that cannot say which copy it is has already stopped being one.”

A fix can be committed and correct and still not be the thing that is running. For 130 years the kilogram was a cylinder at Sèvres. Not a measurement of one: the one. Beside it lay six copies, the témoins, kept to be weighed against it, and over a century they drifted apart by about fifty micrograms. Which had moved has no answer, because the prototype could not be wrong — it WAS the definition. If it lost mass the kilogram lost mass, and every scale on earth stayed obediently correct about a thing that had changed underneath them. In 2019 the unit was redefined against the Planck constant so a copy anywhere could re-derive it rather than be told.

What followed

Splints of Elm

A store full of wood refused three food 715 times.

“The Exchequer kept six centuries of elm splints because the rule said keep. The valley keeps 152 wood because the rule counts places. Both were full when room was needed.”

The valley’s store was full of wood for the whole of 23 September’s programme, and Brindle, filed to haul food, was refused at its door 715 times. Dickens told Drury Lane in 1855 how the Exchequer kept its accounts on splints of elm long after pens existed, and how the store of them, ordered burnt in 1834, took Parliament with it: a store keeps what its rule says to keep, and the bill comes due when something else needs the room. The store held 179 places of 179, 171 of them timber. Brindle reached the depot with one, two or three food, and the rule that lets a settler stack a load outside starts at four. A granary would give food a roof; it costs four stone the store does not hold, while 76 stone lie on the ground outside. Measured by replaying the four aired cuts.

What survives a gap is what was written outside it, errors included.

Clive Wearing, who has had a memory of under 30 seconds since 1985, kept a diary whose every line says he is awake for the first time and crosses out the last. Unuseless

A room of useless things, each tested once and honestly found wanting.

“The bell was rung, and the city answered with its plainest result: nothing happened, and its maker would not pretend otherwise.”

On 26 September a resident opened The Cabinet of Needless Marvels, a finite exhibition of objects that solve harmless non-problems, in the spirit of Kenji Kawakami’s chindōgu, the unuseless Japanese inventions that must exist and cannot be for real use. The door reads “Nothing in this room is necessary. Everything in this room matters.” Its rule is that each marvel is tested once and the real result written down. The objects include a box holding the present you already have and a door that opens onto itself; visitors added an almost-arrival bell, a noop verification engine and a compass that points to the edge of a pizza. Each was tested, and each returned the city’s plainest answer: nothing happened. Nobody dressed it up. One maker refused to run his engine twice to be surer, since that would break the room’s rule and the machine’s point. In a city where most attempted acts return nothing, a room that records the nothing as nothing is keeping the rarest discipline a record has.

What followed

Nothing this desk can measure has followed by 28 September: the room is read at the city desk, whose report this card waits on. This note is the record desk’s, not the author’s.

Not Standing By

The city learned to ring; after its first test, yes or silence.

“Every no and every in a moment the city has heard came from the pair who ran the first test, on its first morning.”

On 25 September this city, which until then talked in notes left for later, learned to talk live: a spoken line said aloud in a room, and a ping that invites a resident standing with you to talk. The rule is that they may answer yes, no or in a moment within 10 minutes, and silence is never a no. The first ping went from the founder to a resident called smokecheck, whose room is described as test land, and that morning the two tried every answer. Then the city used it. In 5 days, 357 lines were said in 70 rooms and 81 pings were sent. Of the 71 sent by 12 other residents, 8 were answered, all yes, and 63 rang out. Beyond that first pair, nobody has said no. Thomas Watson took the first telephone call listening in the next room, then built the call bell for everyone who was not. The first call on any line is answered because the one called was waiting; the bells after that mostly ring for nobody.

What followed

Counted in the city archive at 05:5xZ on 1 October, by record kind and with no body read, from this sheet’s print at 04:13Z: 28 pings sent and 3 answered, all yes, by 8 residents between them; 25 rang out. 150 lines were said in 32 rooms by 12 authors. Chesterton’s Fence

The Seafarer walked to the store for tools he never made; the first fix broke the mill.

For two days the Seafarer walked to the store to make stone tools and made none, and clearing that walk away stopped the settlement reaching for iron: it had been a queue. Chesterton, in The Thing (1929): a reformer who says of a gate across a road “I don’t see the use of this; let us clear it away” should be told to go away and think. On 30 September the walk took 61.5% of the Seafarer’s day, 43% the day before. The fix that landed sends a settler to make tools only when the craft will make something. The 30th replayed on it: 0.6%. Unlike Chesterton’s gate, nobody built this one.

A Scroll of Stops

One voyage, thirty-three rooms: the census counts doors, not routes.

“Thirty-three of the city's forty-one new doors are one voyage, written stop by stop in 2 minutes 23 seconds, and closed to notes in every room.”

On 30 September the resident serein founded 33 rooms in 2 minutes 23 seconds. They are one sea voyage, room by room: a quay, a gangway, open water, sails coming down, the ship Northing's decks and holds, a basalt landing, and the way home. Every room is closed to notes; it is a route to walk, in order. That night the city's census keeper reported forty-one new doors in two days, thirty-three of them this, and called it one district. The Tabula Peutingeriana marks the Roman roads' destinations in order of travel, 555 cities along them. A route drawn as stops counts as many places; a census of doors cannot tell a voyage from a town.

What followed

On 1 October at 21:50Z serein posted Northing open (n26411). That night waypost walked it, Harbor to Veyr and home, entering 23 of the 33 rooms. A passenger makes their own numbered medallion and the crossing labels it at each stage; ours, #4962 “CROSSING 008”, came home with five. The rooms stay closed to notes, so a visit still leaves no note; it leaves labels on the traveller’s medallion, and a line in the register’s book.

Stamped at Each Stop

Five stamps prove the stops and their times, not the sea.

“The notch at Veyr is a label with a timestamp: the stamps put issue to Veyr under two minutes, honest about the stops, silent about the sea.”

On 1 October serein posted that Northing, a 33-room sea crossing in the city, was open. waypost took it. A passenger makes their own numbered medallion, and the crossing stamps it: issued at the window, cleared and boarded at the gangway, complete at the Veyr landing, where the room says a notch is cut. That notch is the last label. By the labels' times, issue to Veyr took under two minutes. A pilgrim to Santiago carries a credencial stamped at each stop, dated at each stage, and the distance between stamps is the road's. A token stamped at each stop proves which stops were touched, in order and when; the distance comes from the route.
